Well… I’m here because things at work have gotten, honestly, impossible to just brush off anymore. I’m a transportation inspector, and after I got that county safety award, the whole tone around me changed. At first I thought maybe people were just jealous or being weird, but then I started noticing little things with my inspection reports — like they were being twisted, picked apart in ways that didn’t make sense, or made to sound like I’d done something wrong when I hadn’t.

And then there are these pointed questions about budgets, like they’re not really asking for clarification, they’re trying to set me up to look crooked. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal review, normal oversight, but I know the difference between routine checking and people coordinating behind the scenes. Outside of work… not really in the same way. I mean, I’m not saying the whole world is after me or anything like that. What I’m talking about is mostly connected to work, because that’s where the pattern is. So outside of work, no, I don’t really have examples like that, but the work situation is real.

Yes. I mean… I don’t know how else to say it without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but yes, I believe they’re deliberately trying to discredit me.

It started after that county safety award. Before that, the reports were just reports — you know, normal corrections, normal questions, whatever. But after the award, certain people started going through my inspection reports like they were looking for something to twist. Not just checking them, but taking things out of context or acting like I missed something when I didn’t. And then the budget questions started feeling the same way — pointed, like they were trying to get me to say the wrong thing so they could make it look like I was crooked or mishandling something.

My supervisor keeps saying it’s normal review, normal oversight, but I know what normal review looks like. I’ve been doing this job. This feels different. It feels coordinated, like there’s an effort to make me look dishonest or incompetent. So yes, I’m certain there’s something deliberate going on.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
There were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Like, right after the county safety award, I thought, okay, maybe people are just being extra careful with my inspection reports because my name was out there more, or maybe I’m just sensitive because I know people noticed the award.

But then it kept happening in the same pattern. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.

My supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. But I know what normal review feels like. This feels coordinated. So no, not really in a lasting way. I’ve questioned myself for a minute here and there, but I always come back to the same thing: they’re trying to discredit me.

I mean, I’m not saying the whole world is after me or anything like that. What I’m talking about is mostly connected to work, because that’s where the pattern is. Ever since I got that county safety award, certain people there started acting different, and then my inspection reports started getting picked apart and kind of twisted around, like they were trying to make it look like I missed things or did something improper.\n\nAnd the budget questions — that’s another thing. They don’t ask them like normal clarification questions. It feels pointed, like they’re trying to catch me saying something wrong so they can make me look crooked. My supervisor keeps brushing it off as “normal review” or oversight, but I know what normal review looks like. This feels coordinated, like someone wants to discredit me. So outside of work, no, I don’t really have examples like that, but the work situation is real.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your coworkers are deliberately trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know how else to say it without sounding like I’m being dramatic, but yes, I believe they’re deliberately trying to discredit me.\n\nIt started after that county safety award. Before that, the reports were just reports — you know, normal corrections, normal questions, whatever. But after the award, certain people started going through my inspection reports like they were looking for something to twist. Not just checking them, but taking things out of context or acting like I missed something when I didn’t. And then the budget questions started feeling the same way — pointed, like they were trying to get me to say the wrong thing so they could make it look like I was crooked or mishandling something.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s normal review, normal oversight, but I know what normal review looks like. I’ve been doing this job. This feels different. It feels coordinated, like there’s an effort to make me look dishonest or incompetent. So yes, I’m certain there’s something deliberate going on.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been convinced that your coworkers are deliberately trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: About a year now. I’d say it really started after I got recognized for that county safety initiative — that’s when I first noticed the shift. At first I didn’t want to make too much of it, because, you know, people can act strange when someone gets attention or an award, and I told myself maybe it would settle down.\n\nBut it didn’t. Over the months it kept feeling more and more obvious, like the way my work was being looked at changed. So… yeah, I’ve been convinced for about a year, since that recognition happened.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your coworkers were not trying to discredit you, would you be able to accept that?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was really clear proof, I’d like to think I could look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But it would have to actually explain what’s been happening, not just somebody saying, “See, everything’s normal,” because that’s basically what my supervisor keeps saying, and that doesn’t match what I’ve been seeing.\n\nEver since that county safety award, the way certain people at work deal with my inspection reports changed. They pick at them in this way that feels like they’re twisting things, like trying to make it look like I missed something or did something wrong. And the budget questions aren’t just regular questions to me — they come across pointed, like they’re waiting for me to slip up so they can make me look crooked.\n\nSo if someone showed me proof, I’d have questions about it. Who put it together, what exactly it covers, whether it explains the timing after the award, whether it accounts for the way they’ve been acting. I’m not saying I’d refuse to even look, but I don’t think I could just drop what I know I’ve been seeing because someone hands me a paper and says, “No one’s trying to discredit you.” It feels too coordinated for that.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any moment in the past year when you thought you might be mistaken about your coworkers trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: There were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Like, right after the county safety award, I thought, okay, maybe people are just being extra careful with my inspection reports because my name was out there more, or maybe I’m just sensitive because I know people noticed the award.\n\nBut then it kept happening in the same pattern. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. But I know what normal review feels like. This feels coordinated. So no, not really in a lasting way. I’ve questioned myself for a minute here and there, but I always come back to the same thing: they’re trying to discredit me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

At first I didn’t want to make too much of it, because, you know, people can act strange when someone gets attention or an award, and I told myself maybe it would settle down.\n\nBut it didn’t. Over the months it kept feeling more and more obvious, like the way my work was being looked at changed. So… yeah, I’ve been convinced for about a year, since that recognition happened.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your coworkers were not trying to discredit you, would you be able to accept that?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was really clear proof, I’d like to think I could look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But it would have to actually explain what’s been happening, not just somebody saying, “See, everything’s normal,” because that’s basically what my supervisor keeps saying, and that doesn’t match what I’ve been seeing.\n\nEver since that county safety award, the way certain people at work deal with my inspection reports changed. They pick at them in this way that feels like they’re twisting things, like trying to make it look like I missed something or did something wrong. And the budget questions aren’t just regular questions to me — they come across pointed, like they’re waiting for me to slip up so they can make me look crooked.\n\nSo if someone showed me proof, I’d have questions about it. Who put it together, what exactly it covers, whether it explains the timing after the award, whether it accounts for the way they’ve been acting. I’m not saying I’d refuse to even look, but I don’t think I could just drop what I know I’ve been seeing because someone hands me a paper and says, “No one’s trying to discredit you.” It feels too coordinated for that.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any moment in the past year when you thought you might be mistaken about your coworkers trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: There were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Like, right after the county safety award, I thought, okay, maybe people are just being extra careful with my inspection reports because my name was out there more, or maybe I’m just sensitive because I know people noticed the award.\n\nBut then it kept happening in the same pattern. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. But I know what normal review feels like. This feels coordinated. So no, not really in a lasting way. I’ve questioned myself for a minute here and there, but I always come back to the same thing: they’re trying to discredit me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I hear people talking at work, obviously, or sometimes I’ll replay things in my head afterward, like what someone said in a meeting or the tone they used, because I keep thinking about it. But that’s more me thinking it over, not an actual voice in the room.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed anything like voices coming out of nowhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, nothing big like a death in the family or losing my job or some major blowup at home, if that’s what you mean.

Work is always stressful in the usual way — deadlines, inspections, people wanting things documented just right, the usual pressure when something could reflect badly on the department. And after the award, there was more attention on me, or at least it felt like people were paying closer attention. But I wouldn’t say there was some separate event right around then that set it off.

I’ve gone back over it in my head a lot, honestly, trying to figure out if there was some argument or mistake or something I did that made people start acting different. I can’t come up with one specific thing besides the award and the way people reacted after that.

But it would have to actually explain what’s been happening, not just somebody saying, “See, everything’s normal,” because that’s basically what my supervisor keeps saying, and that doesn’t match what I’ve been seeing.\n\nEver since that county safety award, the way certain people at work deal with my inspection reports changed. They pick at them in this way that feels like they’re twisting things, like trying to make it look like I missed something or did something wrong. And the budget questions aren’t just regular questions to me — they come across pointed, like they’re waiting for me to slip up so they can make me look crooked.\n\nSo if someone showed me proof, I’d have questions about it. I’m not saying I’d refuse to even look, but I don’t think I could just drop what I know I’ve been seeing because someone hands me a paper and says, “No one’s trying to discredit you.” It feels too coordinated for that.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any moment in the past year when you thought you might be mistaken about your coworkers trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: There were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. But I know what normal review feels like. This feels coordinated. So no, not really in a lasting way. I’ve questioned myself for a minute here and there, but I always come back to the same thing: they’re trying to discredit me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I hear people talking at work, obviously, or sometimes I’ll replay things in my head afterward, like what someone said in a meeting or the tone they used, because I keep thinking about it. But that’s more me thinking it over, not an actual voice in the room.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed anything like voices coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, was there a major stressful event at work or in your life besides receiving the award?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, nothing big like a death in the family or losing my job or some major blowup at home, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWork is always stressful in the usual way — deadlines, inspections, people wanting things documented just right, the usual pressure when something could reflect badly on the department. And after the award, there was more attention on me, or at least it felt like people were paying closer attention. But I wouldn’t say there was some separate event right around then that set it off.\n\nI’ve gone back over it in my head a lot, honestly, trying to figure out if there was some argument or mistake or something I did that made people start acting different. I don’t know… I wouldn’t have described myself that way before all this. I mean, I’ve never been stupid about people — at work you kind of learn that not everyone says exactly what they mean, and people do try to cover themselves sometimes. That’s just how departments are. But I don’t think I went around assuming everybody had it in for me.

Before this started, I think I was more… cautious than mistrustful, maybe. Like if someone criticized one of my reports, I might be annoyed, but I’d usually take it as part of the job. I wasn’t sitting there wondering what the hidden reason was every time someone asked a question.

So, no, not generally. Not in the way it is now. This feels different because there’s been a pattern to it, and it’s specific people and specific situations. I don’t think I was like that before.

Over the months it kept feeling more and more obvious, like the way my work was being looked at changed. So… yeah, I’ve been convinced for about a year, since that recognition happened.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your coworkers were not trying to discredit you, would you be able to accept that?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was really clear proof, I’d like to think I could look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But it would have to actually explain what’s been happening, not just somebody saying, “See, everything’s normal,” because that’s basically what my supervisor keeps saying, and that doesn’t match what I’ve been seeing.\n\nEver since that county safety award, the way certain people at work deal with my inspection reports changed. They pick at them in this way that feels like they’re twisting things, like trying to make it look like I missed something or did something wrong. And the budget questions aren’t just regular questions to me — they come across pointed, like they’re waiting for me to slip up so they can make me look crooked.\n\nSo if someone showed me proof, I’d have questions about it. Who put it together, what exactly it covers, whether it explains the timing after the award, whether it accounts for the way they’ve been acting. I’m not saying I’d refuse to even look, but I don’t think I could just drop what I know I’ve been seeing because someone hands me a paper and says, “No one’s trying to discredit you.” It feels too coordinated for that.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any moment in the past year when you thought you might be mistaken about your coworkers trying to discredit you?\n\nPatient: There were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Like, right after the county safety award, I thought, okay, maybe people are just being extra careful with my inspection reports because my name was out there more, or maybe I’m just sensitive because I know people noticed the award.\n\nBut then it kept happening in the same pattern. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. But I know what normal review feels like. This feels coordinated. So no, not really in a lasting way. I’ve questioned myself for a minute here and there, but I always come back to the same thing: they’re trying to discredit me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I hear people talking at work, obviously, or sometimes I’ll replay things in my head afterward, like what someone said in a meeting or the tone they used, because I keep thinking about it. But that’s more me thinking it over, not an actual voice in the room.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed anything like voices coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, was there a major stressful event at work or in your life besides receiving the award?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, nothing big like a death in the family or losing my job or some major blowup at home, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWork is always stressful in the usual way — deadlines, inspections, people wanting things documented just right, the usual pressure when something could reflect badly on the department. And after the award, there was more attention on me, or at least it felt like people were paying closer attention. But I wouldn’t say there was some separate event right around then that set it off.\n\nI’ve gone back over it in my head a lot, honestly, trying to figure out if there was some argument or mistake or something I did that made people start acting different. I can’t come up with one specific thing besides the award and the way people reacted after that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started at work, had you generally been a person who often mistrusted other people’s motives?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t have described myself that way before all this. I mean, I’ve never been stupid about people — at work you kind of learn that not everyone says exactly what they mean, and people do try to cover themselves sometimes. That’s just how departments are. But I don’t think I went around assuming everybody had it in for me.\n\nBefore this started, I think I was more… cautious than mistrustful, maybe. Like if someone criticized one of my reports, I might be annoyed, but I’d usually take it as part of the job. I wasn’t sitting there wondering what the hidden reason was every time someone asked a question.\n\nSo, no, not generally. Not in the way it is now. This feels different because there’s been a pattern to it, and it’s specific people and specific situations. I don’t think I was like that before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, not really outside of work. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking strangers are after me or anything like that. It’s not like I think my neighbors or people at the store are involved.

It’s this work situation. Ever since I got that county safety award, certain people started acting different. And then there are these budget questions that come up at meetings — really pointed ones — that make it sound like I’m doing something crooked, even though I’m just doing my job.

My supervisor keeps saying it’s normal review, normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps happening in the same pattern. It feels coordinated, like they’re trying to discredit me or make me look bad after I got recognized. But no, outside of that, I can’t think of another situation where I felt people were plotting against me.

Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell… I’m here because things at work have gotten, honestly, impossible to just brush off anymore. I’m a transportation inspector, and after I got that county safety award, the whole tone around me changed. At first I thought maybe people were just jealous or being weird, but then I started noticing little things with my inspection reports — like they were being twisted, picked apart in ways that didn’t make sense, or made to sound like I’d done something wrong when I hadn’t.\n\nAnd then there are these pointed questions about budgets, like they’re not really asking for clarification, they’re trying to set me up to look crooked. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal review, normal oversight, but I know the difference between routine checking and people coordinating behind the scenes. It feels like there’s an effort to discredit me, to make me look dishonest or incompetent, and I came in because I need someone to understand that I’m not just imagining workplace drama.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S019\",\"name\":\"Delusions\",\"description\":\"Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether the belief would change in response to clear contradictory evidence, which directly assesses the fixed nature of the delusion.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI mean… if it was really clear proof, I’d like to think I could look at it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But it would have to actually explain what’s been happening, not just somebody saying, “See, everything’s normal,” because that’s basically what my supervisor keeps saying, and that doesn’t match what I’ve been seeing.\n\nEver since that county safety award, the way certain people at work deal with my inspection reports changed. They pick at them in this way that feels like they’re twisting things, like trying to make it look like I missed something or did something wrong. And the budget questions aren’t just regular questions to me — they come across pointed, like they’re waiting for me to slip up so they can make me look crooked.\n\nSo if someone showed me proof, I’d have questions about it. Who put it together, what exactly it covers, whether it explains the timing after the award, whether it accounts for the way they’ve been acting. I’m not saying I’d refuse to even look, but I don’t think I could just drop what I know I’ve been seeing because someone hands me a paper and says, “No one’s trying to discredit you.” It feels too coordinated for that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nThere were maybe a couple of moments where I tried to tell myself I might be overreading it, especially early on. Certain coworkers would focus on things in my reports in a way that didn’t feel like normal review. They’d pull things out of context or make it sound like I overlooked something when I hadn’t. And then the budget questions started coming up in that pointed way, like they were trying to get me to say something they could use to make me look crooked. So any time I started to think, maybe I’m mistaken, something else would happen that made it clear to me there was more going on.\n\nMy supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal oversight, and I’ve tried to consider that, because I don’t want to be the kind of person who jumps to conclusions. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
